        "text": "All the models leverage specialized knowledge or training strategies to outperform general models. When exploring how to enhance the input representations of LLMs through KGs, several key studies demonstrate various methods to improve the understanding and generation capabilities of the models. CoLAKE (Sun et al., 2020) proposes a unified pre-training framework that jointly learns contextualized representations of language and knowledge by integrating them into a shared structure called the word-knowledge graph. ERNIE 3.0 (Sun et al., 2021b) achieves SOTA on 54 Chinese NLP tasks through hybrid architecture. DKPLM (Zhang T. et al., 2022) improves efficiency in long-tail entity processing via decomposable knowledge injection. JAKET (Yu et al., 2022) enables bidirectional enhancement between knowledge graphs and language. KG-T5 (Moiseev et al., 2022) directly pre-trains on KG triples for 3 \u00d7 performance gain. SAC-KG (Chen S. et al., 2024) leverages LLMs to construct million-scale high-pre\n\n1. Misalignment with natural language semantics: The structured format of KGs often fails to capture the richness and flexibility of natural language. KG-enhanced LLMs frequently struggle to ground unstructured language into these rigid graph structures. This semantic gap leads to poor retrieval of relevant knowledge and ineffective reasoning over the KG. Although recent methods such as joint graph-text embeddings, prompt-based schema alignment, and co-training frameworks have been proposed to bridge this gap, they often require extensive tuning and are task-specific, lacking robust generalization (Peng et al., 2024). 2. Knowledge conflict and redundancy: KGs derived from multiple sources often contain conflicting or redundant facts. For instance, in the biomedical domain, different datasets may offer contradictory treatments for the same disease or disagree on causality between symptoms and conditions.\n\nEvIT (Tao et al., 2024) trains LLMs through event-oriented instruction tuning and uses a heuristic unsupervised method to mine event quadruples from large-scale corpora. Chen R. et al. (2024) uses LLMs as expert annotators to extract event information from sentences and generate augmented datasets aligned with baseline distributions. STAR (Ma M. D. et al., 2024) proposes a data generation method that uses LLMs to synthesize data with minimal seed examples. It generates target structures (Y) and paragraphs (X) through detailed instructions, followed by error identification and iterative improvements to enhance data quality. Entity Linking (EL) matches text mentions to specific entities in a knowledge base to enhance text understanding and information retrieval. ReFinED (Ayoola et al., 2022) uses fine-grained entity types and entity descriptions to construct an efficient end-to-end entity linking model, which can be generalized to other large-scale knowledge bases.\n\nChatEL (Ding Y. et al., 2024) proposes a three-step framework that leverages LLMs for efficient entity linking by generating candidate entities, enhancing contextual information, and incorporating a multiple-choice format. UniMEL (Liu Q. et al., 2024) proposes a multimodal entity linking framework using LLMs. It integrates textual and visual information to enhance mention and entity representations, and improves linking performance through embedding-based retrieval and candidate re-ranking. Coreference resolution is a NLP task that aims to identify and link different expressions in a text that refer to the same entity. Zheng L. et al. (2024) proposes an adaptive multimodal data augmentation framework to tackle data scarcity and under-utilization in multimodal coreference resolution (MCR). Min et al. In: Proceedings of the 22nd USENIX Conference on Security, pp.\n\nIn the field of Internet of Things (IoT) security, tasks such as network asset discovery and threat intelligence typically rely on IP addresses to identify target devices. However, the dynamic nature of IP addresses leads to identity distortion, whereas device fingerprinting can extract highly distinctive features of target devices, offers an effective solution to this issue. Existing device fingerprinting methods often suffer from limited applicability and poor generalization capability, making them less effective in large-scale Internet environments. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a novel device fingerprint discovery approach based on fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).         "text": "This method involves using a fixed LLM to understand and process the semantic content of the prompt through zero-shot capabilities. Following this, it integrates the processed prompt with the input text to improve the model\u2019s performance on ...\n\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) are gaining significant popularity in recent years for specialized tasks using prompts due to their low computational cost. Standard methods like prefix tuning utilize special, modifiable tokens that lack semantic meaning and require extensive training for best performance, often falling short. In this context, we propose a novel method called Semantic Knowledge Tuning (SK-Tuning) for prompt and prefix tuning that employs meaningful words instead of random tokens. This method involves using a fixed LLM to understand and process the semantic content of the prompt through zero-shot capabilities. Following this, it integrates the processed prompt with the input text to improve the model\u2019s performance on particular tasks. Our experimental results show that SK-Tuning exhibits faster training times, fewer parameters, and superior performance on tasks such as text classification and understanding compared to other tuning methods.\n\nScientific Reports - Parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models using semantic knowledge tuning\n\nHern\u00e1ndez, A., Ortega-Mendoza, R. Prompt tuning8 does not modify the underlying structure of the model, potentially resulting in quicker inference times and decreased resource consumption in contrast to utilizing adapters. Furthermore, prefix tuning9 has been proposed as a method to improve performance. Unlike prompt tuning, which updates only a portion of a single layer, prefix tuning updates a section of every layer consistently, and it has shown improved performance in succeeding tasks.         "text": "Finally, the best-performing model is selected and validated on the test set to estimate its generalization ability. ... Overall experimentation&#x27;s process. In this phase, we directly utilize the WebNLG and KELM-sub datasets as provided by Han et al., without additional modifications. These datasets have been carefully curated by experts to align with the T2KG task, ensuring high-quality, manually verified triples for knowledge graph construction. Given their structured nature, no further preprocessing was required before feeding them into our pipeline. However, while these datasets provide a strong foundation, future work could explore entity harmonization techniques to further refine the extracted triples. By integrating entity linking strategies (Dredze et al., 2010) or LLM-assisted canonicalization methods (Zhang and Soh, 2024), we could improve coherence and reduce redundancy in the constructed knowledge graphs.\n\nFinally, the best-performing model is selected and validated on the test set to estimate its generalization ability. ... Overall experimentation's process. In this phase, we directly utilize the WebNLG and KELM-sub datasets as provided by Han et al., without additional modifications. These datasets have been carefully curated by experts to align with the T2KG task, ensuring high-quality, manually verified triples for knowledge graph construction. Given their structured nature, no further preprocessing was required before feeding them into our pipeline. However, while these datasets provide a strong foundation, future work could explore entity harmonization techniques to further refine the extracted triples. By integrating entity linking strategies (Dredze et al., 2010) or LLM-assisted canonicalization methods (Zhang and Soh, 2024), we could improve coherence and reduce redundancy in the constructed knowledge graphs.\n\nWe also plan to conduct further experiments with newer LLM versions and investigate the impact of hyperparameter tuning on fine-tuning and generalization performance. Finally, while this study benchmarks various LLM-based approaches for knowledge graph construction, a comparative analysis with traditional NLP-based techniques remains an important area of investigation. Evaluating LLM-based methods alongside classical rule-based or statistical approaches could provide deeper insights into their relative advantages. We leave this exploration as a promising direction for future research. The authors thank the French company DAVI (Davi The Humanizers, Puteaux, France) for their support, and the French government for the plan France Relance funding. The content of this manuscript has been presented in part at the 3rd International Workshop on Knowledge Graph Generation from Text (Text2KG), co-located with ESWC 2024 (Ghanem and Cruz, 2024).\n\nEvaluation metrics used to assess the quality of constructed KGs are also discussed, including semantic consistency and linguistic coherence. This section highlights methods and metrics to construct KGs and evaluate the result. (2024) evaluate the performance of LLMs, specifically GPT-4 and ChatGPT, in KG construction and reasoning tasks, introducing the Virtual Knowledge Extraction task and the VINE dataset, but t\n\nIn our approach for ZSP, we began with the methodology outlined in Han et al. (2023), initiating our prompt with the directive \u201cTransform the text into a semantic graph.\u201d However, we enhanced this prompt by incorporating additional sentences tailored for our LLMs, as illustrated in Figure 3a. The effectiveness FSP depends heavily on example selection (Yoshida, 2024). In our experiments, we selected examples based on criteria shown in the following paragraph. However, prior research suggests that structured selection methods\u2013such as clustering diverse examples or prioritizing domain-specific samples\u2013could significantly improve performance. Exploring optimal example selection strategies remains a valuable direction for future work. For FSP, we executed 7-shots learning. The rationale behind employing 7-shots learning lies in the fact that the maximum KG size in WebNLG+2020 is 7 triples.",

        "text": "Consequently, using external datasets to incorporate new information or refine the capabilities of LLMs on previously seen information poses a significant challenge. In this study, we compare two common approaches: unsupervised fine-tuning and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We evaluate both approaches on a variety of knowledge-intensive tasks across different topics. Our findings reveal that while unsupervised fine-tuning offers some improvement, RAG consistently outperforms it, both for existing knowledge encountered during training and entirely new knowledge. Moreover, we find that LLMs struggle to learn new factual information through unsupervised fine-tuning, and that exposing them to numerous variations of the same fact during training could alleviate this problem. ... Oded Ovadia, Menachem Brief, Moshik Mishaeli, and Oren Elisha. 2024.\n\nConsequently, using external datasets to incorporate new information or refine the capabilities of LLMs on previously seen information poses a significant challenge. In this study, we compare two common approaches: unsupervised fine-tuning and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We evaluate both approaches on a variety of knowledge-intensive tasks across different topics. Our findings reveal that while unsupervised fine-tuning offers some improvement, RAG consistently outperforms it, both for existing knowledge encountered during training and entirely new knowledge. Moreover, we find that LLMs struggle to learn new factual information through unsupervised fine-tuning, and that exposing them to numerous variations of the same fact during training could alleviate this problem. ...         "title": "SIGIR '24: Proceedings of the 47th International ACM SIGIR Conference on Research and Development in Information Retrieval",

        "text": "In reality, user sessions can vary, where the number of intentions may differ from one to another. Moreover, they can only learn user intents in the latent space, which further restricts the model&#x27;s transparency. To ease these issues, we propose a simple yet effective paradigm for ISR motivated by the advanced reasoning capability of large language models (LLMs). Specifically, we first create an initial prompt to instruct LLMs to predict the next item by inferring varying user intents reflected in a session. Then, we propose an effective optimization mechanism to automatically optimize prompts with an iterative self-reflection. Finally, we leverage the robust generalizability of LLMs across diverse domains to efficiently select the optimal prompt for ISR. As such, the proposed paradigm effectively guides LLMs to identify varying user intents at a semantic level, thus delivering ...\n\nIn reality, user sessions can vary, where the number of intentions may differ from one to another. Moreover, they can only learn user intents in the latent space, which further restricts the model's transparency. To ease these issues, we propose a simple yet effective paradigm for ISR motivated by the advanced reasoning capability of large language models (LLMs). Specifically, we first create an initial prompt to instruct LLMs to predict the next item by inferring varying user intents reflected in a session. Then, we propose an effective optimization mechanism to automatically optimize prompts with an iterative self-reflection. Finally, we leverage the robust generalizability of LLMs across diverse domains to efficiently select the optimal prompt for ISR. As such, the proposed paradigm effectively guides LLMs to identify varying user intents at a semantic level, thus delivering more accurate and comprehensible recommendations.\n\nSeveral large language model (LLM) agents have been constructed for diverse purposes such as web navigation and online shopping, leveraging the broad knowledge and text comprehension capabilities of LLMs. Many of these works rely on in-context examples to achieve generalization without requiring fine-tuning. However, few have addressed the challenge of selecting and effectively utilizing these examples. Recent approaches have introduced trajectory-level retrieval with task meta-data and the use of trajectories as in-context examples to enhance overall performance in some sequential decision making tasks like computer control. Nevertheless, these methods face issues like plausible examples retrieved without task-specific state transition dynamics and long input with plenty of irrelevant context due to using complete trajectories. In this paper, we propose a novel framework (TRAD) to tackle these problems.\n\nThen, Aligned Decision is introduced to complement retrieved demonstration steps with their preceding or subsequent steps, providing tolerance for imperfect thought and offering a balance between more context and less noise. Extensive experiments on ALFWorld and Mind2Web benchmarks demonstrate that TRAD not only surpasses state-of-the-art models but also effectively reduces noise and promotes generalization. Furthermore, TRAD has been deployed in real-world scenarios of a global business insurance company and yields an improved success rate of robotic process automation. Our codes are available at: https://github.com/skyriver-2000/TRAD-Official. ... With the increasing ability of large language models (LLMs), in-context learning (ICL) has evolved as a new paradigm for natural language processing (NLP), where instead of fine- tuning the parameters of an LLM specific to a downstream task with labeled examples,a small number of such examples is appended to a prompt instruction for control\n\nRLT is crucial for re-ranking as it can improve re-ranking efficiency by sending variable-length candidate lists to a re-ranker on a per-query basis. It also has the potential to improve re-ranking effectiveness. Despite its importance, there is limited research into applying RLT methods to this new perspective. To address this research gap, we reproduce existing RLT methods in the context of re-ranking, especially newly emerged large language model (LLM)-based re-ranking. In particular, we examine to what extent established findings on RLT for retrieval are generalizable to the \"retrieve-then-re-rank\" setup from three perspectives: (i) assessing RLT methods in the context of LLM-based re-ranking with lexical first-stage retrieval, (ii) investigating the impact of different types of first-stage retrievers on RLT methods, and (iii) investigating the impact of different types of re-rankers on RLT methods.",
        "is_citable": true,

        "type": "knowledge",
        "title": "",
        "url": "https://aclanthology.org/2024.emnlp-main.15.pdf",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",
          "site_domain": "aclanthology.org",

        "text": "Proceedings of the 2024 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, pages 237\u2013250\r\nNovember 12-16, 2024 \u00a92024 Association for Computational Linguistics\r\nFine-Tuning or Retrieval? Comparing Knowledge Injection in LLMs\r\nOded Ovadia*, Meni Brief*, Moshik Mishaeli, and Oren Elisha\r\nMicrosoft, Israel\r\nAbstract\r\nLarge language models (LLMs) encapsulate\r\na vast amount of factual information within\r\ntheir pre-trained weights, as evidenced by their\r\nability to answer diverse questions across dif\u0002ferent domains. However, this knowledge is\r\ninherently limited, relying heavily on the char\u0002acteristics of the training data. Consequently,\r\nusing external datasets to incorporate new in\u0002formation or refine the capabilities of LLMs\r\non previously seen information poses a sig\u0002nificant challenge. In this study, we com\u0002pare two common approaches: unsupervised\r\nfine-tuning and retrieval-augmented generation\r\n(RAG). We evaluate both approaches on a vari\u0002ety of knowledge-intensive tasks across differ\u0002ent topics. Our findings reveal that while unsu\u0002pervised fine-tuning offers some improvement,\r\nRAG consistently outperforms it, both for ex\u0002isting knowledge encountered during training\r\nand entirely new knowledge. Moreover, we\r\nfind that LLMs struggle to learn new factual\r\ninformation through unsupervised fine-tuning,\r\nand that exposing them to numerous variations\r\nof the same fact during training could alleviate\r\nthis problem.\r\n1 Introduction\r\nLarge language models (LLMs) are able to cap\u0002ture vast amounts of factual information (Petroni\r\net al., 2019; Cohen et al., 2023; Hu et al., 2023).\r\nLLMs exhibit a remarkable level of knowledge in\r\nvarious domains due to their massive pre-training\r\ndatasets. However, there are two significant limita\u0002tions to this knowledge. First, it is static and does\r\nnot update with time. Second, it is non-specific\r\nand thus may lack nuanced expertise in particular\r\ndomains. While these are two different problems,\r\nthey are deeply related since their solution is the\r\nsame: enhancing the model\u2019s knowledge.\r\nRecently, the idea of adapting LLMs to partic\u0002ular domains and updating their knowledge has\r\n*Equal contribution.\r\nbecome increasingly common (Yu et al., 2022).\r\nVarious models have been suggested to improve\r\nfactual knowledge and capabilities in diverse fields\r\nsuch as healthcare (Singhal et al., 2023a,b; Wu\r\net al., 2023a), finance (Wu et al., 2023b; Yang et al.,\r\n2023), and law (Huang et al., 2023; Nguyen, 2023).\r\nIn this work, we focus on the evaluation of a\r\nmodel\u2019s knowledge and its ability to memorize,\r\nunderstand, and retrieve factual data. We aim to un\u0002derstand the concept of knowledge injection (Wang\r\net al., 2020; Chen et al., 2022; Liu et al., 2020;\r\nLauscher et al., 2020). Given some knowledge\r\nbase in the form of a text corpus, what is the best\r\nway to teach a pre-trained model this knowledge?\r\nOne way to add knowledge to a pre-trained\r\nmodel is through fine-tuning. With fine-tuning,\r\nwe continue the model\u2019s training process and adapt\r\nit using task-specific data. By exposing the model\r\nto a specific knowledge base, we expect the model\r\nweights to adapt accordingly. This process is meant\r\nto optimize the model for targeted applications, en\u0002hancing its performance and contextual relevance\r\nin specialized domains.\r\nAnother method to enhance a model\u2019s knowl\u0002edge base is through the use of in-context learning\r\n(ICL) (Chen et al., 2021; Radford et al., 2019; Min\r\net al., 2021; Lampinen et al., 2022). The main idea\r\nbehind ICL is to improve the performance of pre\u0002trained LLMs on new tasks by modifying the input\r\nquery to the model without directly changing the\r\nweights of the model. One form of ICL is retrieval\r\naugmented generation (RAG) (Lewis et al., 2020;\r\nNeelakantan et al., 2022). RAG uses information\r\nretrieval techniques to enable LLMs to obtain rel\u0002evant information from a knowledge source and\r\nincorporate it into generated text.\r\nThis study aims to evaluate the knowledge injec\u0002tion capabilities of LLMs through a comparison of\r\nfine-tuning and RAG. To illustrate the rationale, let\r\nus use an analogy. Consider three college students\r\ntaking a test on a specific topic. All had access\r\n237\nto class materials but didn\u2019t know the topic before\u0002hand. The first student had the textbook only during\r\nthe test, the second had pre-test access and studied,\r\nand the third lost access upon the test announce\u0002ment. Who would probably perform better?\r\n2 Background\r\nTo assess knowledge injection, we must first under\u0002stand what knowledge means for LLMs.\r\nKnowledge and Language Models Defining\r\nknowledge is a complex philosophical task far be\u0002yond the scope of this research. However, we can\r\nexamine what factual knowledge means in the con\u0002text of language models. If a model knows a fact,\r\nit can accurately and consistently answer questions\r\nabout it. Furthermore, it can reliably distinguish\r\nbetween true and false statements related to this\r\nfact. We can then extend this definition to a whole\r\nknowledge base, not just a single fact.\r\nMathematically, let Q = {qn}\r\nN\r\nn=1 be a set of\r\nN multiple choice factual questions, where each\r\nquestion has L possible answers and exactly one\r\ncorrect answer. Let A = {(a\r\n1\r\nn\r\n, . . . , aL\r\nn\r\n)}\r\nN\r\nn=1 be\r\nthe corresponding set of possible answers, and C =\r\n{cn}\r\nN\r\nn=1 be the correct answers.\r\nLet M be a language model. We denote by\r\nM(qn) \u2208 {a\r\n1\r\nn\r\n, . . . , aL\r\nn} the predicted answer of\r\nthe model to the n-th question. We define the\r\nknowledge score L of M in relation to Q to be\r\nthe standard accuracy score:\r\nLM,Q :=\r\n#{qn| M(qn) = cn}\r\nN\r\n. (1)\r\nWe say that the model M possesses any knowl\u0002edge regarding the set of questions Q if the follow\u0002ing holds:\r\nLM,Q >\r\n1\r\nL\r\n. (2)\r\nIn simpler terms, the model can consistently give\r\ncorrect answers, outperforming a simple random\r\nguessing baseline. Naturally, if the knowledge\r\nscore LM,Q is higher for one model compared to\r\nanother, then we assert that the former is more\r\nknowledgeable with regards to Q compared to the\r\nlatter.\r\nPreviously Seen Knowledge One important\r\ndistinction to make is between knowledge that\r\nthe model has been exposed to before during pre\u0002training as opposed to entirely new facts. Con\u0002sidering the size of modern LLM training sets,\r\nthey cover a vast amount of information available\r\nthrough web-sourced text. As a result, even in\r\nniche domains, the goal of knowledge injection\r\nis not necessarily to teach the model entirely new\r\nfacts but rather to \"refresh\" its memory by inducing\r\na bias toward a particular domain.\r\nKnowledge and Reasoning We emphasize\r\nthat this knowledge evaluation framework for\r\nLLMs is imperfect. Importantly, it doesn\u2019t ad\u0002dress other quality metrics influencing a model\u2019s\r\nresponse. Creating a purely knowledge-intensive\r\ndataset without involving some level of reasoning\r\nis challenging. Consequently, a model with ro\u0002bust reasoning abilities might excel on unfamiliar\r\nknowledge-intensive tasks by making \"educated\r\nguesses\" in a multiple-choice exam. Therefore, any\r\nevaluation of knowledge in LLMs should consider\r\nthis, with results seen as part of a broader range of\r\nbenchmarks for reasoning (Sakaguchi et al., 2021),\r\nreading comprehension (Dua et al., 2019), and\r\ngeneral language abilities (Srivastava et al., 2022).\r\nHowever, this evaluation framework still strongly\r\nemphasizes factual information above all else.\r\nCauses for Factual Errors There are many\r\npossible reasons for the failure of models to answer\r\nfactual questions accurately. In (Wang et al., 2023),\r\nWang et al. introduce a taxonomy of five main\r\nmodel-level causes:\r\n\u2022 Domain knowledge deficit: A language model\r\nmay lack comprehensive expertise in a specific\r\ndomain to which it has not been exposed. For\r\nexample, a model trained exclusively on texts\r\nwritten by William Shakespeare would perform\r\npoorly when asked about the works of Mark\r\nTwain.\r\n\u2022 Outdated Information: LLMs invariably have\r\na cutoff date determined by their training\r\ndataset. Consequently, any events, discoveries,\r\nor changes occurring after the last training up\u0002date will not be within the model\u2019s knowledge\r\nwithout access to external sources.\r\n\u2022 Immemorization: Sometimes, a model is ex\u0002posed to knowledge during its training process\r\nbut does not retain it. This is especially true for\r\nrare facts that appear in the training dataset only\r\nscarcely (Kandpal et al., 2023).\r\n\u2022 Forgetting: Language models often undergo\r\nadditional training after the pre-training phase\r\n(fine-tuning). In some cases, this might lead\r\nto a phenomenon called catastrophic forgetting\r\n238\nFigure 1: A visualization of the knowledge injection framework.\r\n(Kirkpatrick et al., 2017; Goodfellow et al., 2013;\r\nChen et al., 2020; Luo et al., 2023), where mod\u0002els lose some of the knowledge they had prior to\r\nthe fine-tuning process.\r\n\u2022 Reasoning Failure: In certain instances, a lan\u0002guage model might possess relevant knowledge\r\nabout a fact but fail to utilize it properly. This is\r\nparticularly evident in complex multi-step reason\u0002ing tasks (Tan et al., 2023) or when posed with\r\ndifferent questions about the same fact, resulting\r\nin disparate outcomes (Berglund et al., 2023).\r\nWe observe that most of these issues arise during\r\nthe pre-training phase, with catastrophic forgetting\r\nbeing the notable exception. Hence, many LLMs\r\nwill suffer from factual errors of this kind regard\u0002less of any post-training process.\r\n3 Injecting Knowledge to Language\r\nModels\r\nFollowing the background given in Section 2, it\r\nis clear that general pre-training is insufficient for\r\nmany knowledge-intensive tasks. To solve this,\r\nan additional post-processing step is essential to\r\naugment the knowledge of a pre-trained model.\r\nThis step is often reffered to as knowledge injection\r\n(Wang et al., 2020; Chen et al., 2022; Liu et al.,\r\n2020; Lauscher et al., 2020).\r\nIn this section, we examine two widely used\r\nframeworks for knowledge injection: fine-tuning\r\n(FT) and retrieval augmented generation (RAG).\r\nWe begin by formulating the knowledge injection\r\nproblem, aiming to explain both methods using\r\nconsistent terminology.\r\n3.1 Problem formulation\r\nIn Equations (1) and (2), we presented a formu\u0002lation for knowledge in language models through\r\nthe lens of question-answering (Q&A). We now ex\u0002tend this formulation to the problem of knowledge\r\ninjection using the same terminology.\r\nGiven a set of factual questions, there exists\r\nsome text corpus containing information that is\r\nrelevant to these questions. The central assumption\r\nof knowledge injection is that given full access to\r\nthis corpus, it could serve as an auxiliary knowl\u0002edge base and improve the model\u2019s performance\r\non this set of questions.\r\nMathematically, let M be a pre-trained model,\r\nand let Q be a set of factual questions, as before.\r\nNow, assume we have a relevant auxiliary knowl\u0002edge base BQ. Our objective is to discover a trans\u0002formation, denoted as F, that, when applied, would\r\nenhance the knowledge about Q:\r\nM\u2032:= F(M, BQ) s.t. LM\u2032\r\n,Q > LM,Q. (3)\r\nIn this work, we aim to compare two choices\r\nfor F: fine-tuning and RAG to see which option\r\nperforms better in this problem.\r\n239\n3.2 Fine-Tuning\r\nFine-tuning is the process of adjusting a pre-trained\r\nmodel on a specific, often narrower, dataset or task\r\nto enhance its performance in that particular do\u0002main. Here, it is vital to distinguish between dif\u0002ferent types of fine-tuning. FT techniques are com\u0002monly classified into supervised, unsupervised, and\r\nreinforcement learning (RL) based methods. We\r\nproceed by briefly reviewing these methods and\r\ntheir relation to the problem of knowledge injec\u0002tion.\r\nSupervised Fine-Tuning Supervised fine\u0002tuning (SFT) requires sets of labeled input-output\r\npairs. One of the most common SFT methods\r\nis instruction tuning (Wang et al., 2022; Mishra\r\net al., 2021; Ouyang et al., 2022; Taori et al., 2023),\r\nwhich has emerged as one of the most powerful\r\nmethods to improve model performance. With in\u0002struction tuning, the input is a natural language\r\ntask description, and the output is an example of\r\nthe desired behavior. Many current state-of-the-art\r\nLLMs have gone through instruction tuning after\r\ntheir pre-training phase.\r\nInstruction tuning has been shown to be very\r\neffective at improving the overall quality of the\r\nmodel, with a particular emphasis on its zero-shot\r\nand reasoning capabilities. However, despite these\r\nadvantages, instruction tuning does not necessarily\r\nteach the model new knowledge (Ouyang et al.,\r\n2022; Chung et al., 2022; Mitra et al., 2023; Chia\r\net al., 2023; Zhou et al., 2023). As such, instruc\u0002tion tuning alone is not a viable solution to the\r\nknowledge injection problem.\r\nReinforcement Learning Another form of\r\nFT relies on RL or RL-inspired optimization strate\u0002gies to better align the model after its pre-training\r\nphase. A few prominent examples are reinforce\u0002ment learning from human feedback (RLHF) (Ope\u0002nAI, 2023; Touvron et al., 2023), direct preference\r\noptimization (DPO) (Rafailov et al., 2023), and\r\nproximal policy optimization (PPO) (Schulman\r\net al., 2017; Tunstall et al., 2023).\r\nThese techniques have been shown to be very\r\nuseful, especially when used in conjunction with in\u0002struction tuning. However, similarly to instruction\r\ntuning, these methods focus on the overall quality\r\nof the response and its expected behavior and not\r\nnecessarily on its breadth of knowledge.\r\nUnsupervised Fine-Tuning The final FT\r\nstrategy we discuss is unsupervised, meaning there\r\nare no available labels for the model to learn from.\r\nOne common unsupervised FT technique is often\r\nreferred to as continual pre-training or unstruc\u0002tured FT.\r\nIn this method, the FT process is viewed as a\r\ndirect continuation of the pre-training phase. We\r\nstart with a saved checkpoint of the original LLM\r\nand train it in a causal auto-regressive manner, i.e.,\r\npredicting the next token. One major difference in\r\ncomparison to actual pre-training is the learning\r\nrate. Usually, one would need a much lower learn\u0002ing rate when continuing the pre-training of the\r\nmodel to avoid catastrophic forgetting (Kirkpatrick\r\net al., 2017).\r\nIt is well known that LLMs store vast amounts\r\nof knowledge during their pre-training phase (Zhou\r\net al., 2023). So, it makes sense to continue\r\nthis process in order to inject knowledge into the\r\nmodel. Hence, we use the unsupervised FT ap\u0002proach throughout this work and evaluate its effi\u0002cacy in enhancing the model\u2019s capacity for learning\r\nnew information.\r\n3.3 Retrieval Augmented Generation\r\nRetrieval augmented generation (RAG) (Lewis\r\net al., 2020) is a technique that expands LLMs\u2019 ca\u0002pabilities, especially in knowledge-intensive tasks,\r\nby using external knowledge sources. While the\r\noriginal formulation involved additional training\r\nper task, it has since been demonstrated (Neelakan\u0002tan et al., 2022) that a pre-trained embedding model\r\ncan achieve improved performance with no addi\u0002tional training involved.\r\nThe idea is that given an auxiliary knowledge\r\nbase and an input query, we use the RAG architec\u0002ture to find documents within the knowledge base\r\nthat resemble the input query. These documents are\r\nthen added to the input query, thus giving the model\r\nfurther context about the subject of the query.\r\nIn practice, implementing the suggested archi\u0002tecture is quite straightforward: Given an auxiliary\r\nknowledge base BQ and a pre-trained embedding\r\nmodel Me, we create a dense vector representation\r\n(embedding) per document b \u2208 BQ and store these\r\nin a vector store. Upon receiving a new query q, we\r\nuse its embedding, Me(q), to retrieve q\u2019s top-K\r\nclosest neighbors, bq = {bk}\r\nK\r\n1\r\n, according to dot\u0002product ranking. We then update q to be q\u02dc = bq\u2225q,\r\nwhere \u2225 denotes string concatenation. Finally, we\r\nreturn M(\u02dcq) as the model\u2019s output.\r\n240\nTable 1: Results for the MMLU datasets described in Section 4.1 in terms of log-likelihood accuracy (Equation (4)).\r\nTask Model Base model Base model + RAG Fine-tuned Fine-tuned + RAG\r\nAnatomy (0-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.556 0.681 0.570 0.659\r\nLlama2 7B 0.393 0.489 0.430 0.489\r\nOrca2 7B 0.607 0.637 0.600 0.637\r\nAnatomy (5-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.600 0.681 0.622 0.674\r\nLlama2 7B 0.467 0.563 0.496 0.548\r\nOrca2 7B 0.570 0.659 0.593 0.674\r\nAstronomy (0-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.625 0.678 0.651 0.697\r\nLlama2 7B 0.401 0.467 0.487 0.520\r\nOrca2 7B 0.645 0.750 0.651 0.750\r\nAstronomy (5-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.658 0.724 0.651 0.697\r\nLlama2 7B 0.401 0.474 0.447 0.520\r\nOrca2 7B 0.664 0.763 0.664 0.743\r\nCollege biology (0-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.681 0.757 0.701 0.764\r\nLlama2 7B 0.438 0.493 0.458 0.465\r\nOrca2 7B 0.583 0.639 0.604 0.632\r\nCollege biology (5-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.722 0.778 0.736 0.771\r\nLlama2 7B 0.451 0.521 0.424 0.479\r\nOrca2 7B 0.604 0.660 0.625 0.653\r\nCollege chemistry (0-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.470 0.500 0.490 0.500\r\nLlama2 7B 0.310 0.380 0.390 0.390\r\nOrca2 7B 0.370 0.440 0.370 0.390\r\nCollege chemistry (5-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.470 0.540 0.500 0.500\r\nLlama2 7B 0.370 0.380 0.360 0.390\r\nOrca2 7B 0.430 0.470 0.370 0.380\r\nPrehistory (0-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.713 0.750 0.719 0.731\r\nLlama2 7B 0.448 0.481 0.457 0.478\r\nOrca2 7B 0.642 0.679 0.673 0.673\r\nPrehistory (5-shot)\r\nMistral 7B 0.722 0.762 0.725 0.762\r\nLlama2 7B 0.515 0.531 0.503 0.537\r\nOrca2 7B 0.664 0.698 0.667 0.694\r\nTable 2: Current events results. Models that were fine-tuned on the original dataset are labeled as FT-reg, while\r\nthose trained on the dataset with multiple paraphrases are labeled as FT-par.\r\nBase model Base model + RAG FT-reg FT-par FT-reg + RAG FT-par + RAG\r\nMistral 7B 0.481 0.875 0.504 0.588 0.810 0.830\r\nLlama2 7B 0.353 0.585 0.219 0.392 0.326 0.520\r\nOrca2 7B 0.456 0.876 0.511 0.566 0.820 0.826\r\n4 Knowledge Base Creation\r\n4.1 Task Selection and Rationale\r\nMMLU Benchmark To properly evaluate the\r\ncapabilities of LLMs on knowledge-intensive tasks,\r\nwe selected four distinct tasks from the Massively\r\nMultilingual Language Understanding Evaluation\r\n(MMLU) benchmark (Hendrycks et al., 2021) in\r\nthe topics of anatomy, astronomy, college biology,\r\ncollege chemistry and prehistory. The chosen tasks\r\nwere selected based on their emphasis on factual\r\nknowledge and the minimal reliance on reasoning.\r\nAs a heuristic, we opted for tasks where the ques\u0002tions are short and involve no context. In practice\r\nwe selected four STEM subjects as well as one hu\u0002manities subject, to ensure the evaluation is not lim\u0002ited to certain fields. Note that prehistory involves\r\nquestions spanning all non-modern history. This\r\napproach aims to enable us to test LLM proficiency\r\nin comprehending and manipulating information in\r\n241\nisolation from its reasoning processes.\r\nCurrent Events Task To further isolate\r\nLLMs\u2019 abilities to learn new knowledge, we cre\u0002ated a task comprising multiple-choice questions\r\nabout current events. This task includes multiple\u0002choice questions about events that occurred after\r\nthe cutoff of the various models\u2019 training data.\r\nSpecifically, we focused on \"current events\" from\r\nthe USA, in the time span of August-November\r\n2023, that are included in the relevant Wikipedia\r\nindexes1. This method enables us to mostly guaran\u0002tee that the models have not been exposed to these\r\nfacts, thus allowing us to directly test knowledge\r\ninjection capabilities.\r\n4.2 Data Collection and Preprocessing\r\nTo effectively evaluate the LLMs\u2019 performance on\r\nthese knowledge-intensive tasks, a comprehensive\r\nauxiliary dataset was collected by scraping relevant\r\narticles per topic from Wikipedia. The rationale be\u0002hind selecting Wikipedia as the primary source of\r\nknowledge is its broad coverage of relevant topics\r\nand its reliability as a repository of crowd-verified\r\nknowledge. All articles pertinent to the tasks were\r\nretrieved via the official Wikipedia API2 by identi\u0002fying the relevant central page per topic.\r\nSubsequently, a rigorous cleaning process was\r\nutilized to transform the data from raw subsec\u0002tions to clean chunks. This step was done with\r\nthe \"wikiextractor\" tool (Attardi, 2015). The divi\u0002sion into small, clean (e.g., remove HTML, URLs,\r\netc.) chunks was aimed at enhancing the evalu\u0002ation of the LLMs\u2019 understanding across various\r\nknowledge domains and aiding the LLMs in the\r\nfine-tuning process.\r\n4.3 Current Events Task Creation\r\nAfter collecting the relevant chunks from\r\nWikipedia, we created a new multiple-choice\r\ndataset with the help of GPT-4 (OpenAI, 2023).\r\nFirst, we removed any small chunks. For each\r\nremaining chunk in the corpus, GPT-4 was in\u0002structed to create four highly specific, high-quality\r\nmultiple-choice questions with only one correct\r\nanswer. By specific, we mean that the question\r\ncan be answered without knowledge of which\r\ncontext the question refers to and with minimal\r\nambiguity. Next, GPT-4 was asked to select the\r\ntwo most specific of the four. This was followed\r\n1\r\nhttps://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:\r\n2023_events_in_the_United_States_by_month\r\n2\r\nhttps://www.mediawiki.org/wiki/API:Main_page\r\nby a manual evaluation and verification step. In\r\ntotal, this resulted in 910 new questions.\r\n4.4 Paraphrases Generation\r\nAfter creating the dataset, we utilized GPT-4 to gen\u0002erate augmentations of the dataset. We instructed\r\nGPT-4 to provide paraphrased versions of the input\r\ndata that fully retain the information while being\r\nreworded. Each paraphrasing iteration was done\r\nwith a different seed to ensure variety.\r\nWe selected 240 chunks at random for each task\r\nand created two paraphrases per chunk. These were\r\nset aside to be used as validation sets for hyperpa\u0002rameter tuning. For the current events dataset, we\r\ncreated ten paraphrases for each chunk used in the\r\nfine-tuning process described in Section 6.\r\n5 Experiments and Results\r\nExperimental Framework We used the popular\r\nLM-Evaluation-Harness (Gao et al., 2021) reposi\u0002tory to evaluate the performance of LLMs on the se\u0002lected knowledge-intensive tasks. LM-Evaluation\u0002Harness is a robust benchmarking tool that cur\u0002rently serves as the industry standard for model\r\nevaluation and is the basis of the HuggingFace\r\nleaderboard3. Leveraging this platform ensured\r\na standardized evaluation framework and allowed\r\nconsistent comparison across models, methods, and\r\ndatasets. More importantly, by using the industry\r\nstandard for evaluation, we could avoid any dif\u0002ferences stemming from prompt engineering and\r\nformatting issues and replicate the reported base\u0002line results for each model.\r\nModel Selection We chose three models for\r\ninference evaluation: Llama2-7B (Touvron et al.,\r\n2023), Mistral-7B (Jiang et al., 2023), and Orca2-\r\n7B (Mitra et al., 2023). The choice of these mod\u0002els was meant to represent the most popular open\u0002source base models and an instruction-tuned model\r\nacross various baseline capabilities. Additionally,\r\nwe selected bge-large-en (Xiao et al., 2023) as the\r\nembedding model for the RAG component and\r\nused FAISS (Johnson et al., 2019) as its vector\u0002store. This embedding model is currently the SOTA\r\nof open-source embedding models, according to\r\nthe HuggingFace MTEB leaderboard4.\r\nConfiguration Variations Our evaluation in\u0002cluded multiple configurations, with a grid-search\r\n3\r\nhttps://huggingface.co/spaces/HuggingFaceH4/\r\nopen_llm_leaderboard\r\n4\r\nhttps://huggingface.co/spaces/mteb/\r\nleaderboard\r\n242\nFigure 2: The relative accuracy gain (as explained\r\nin Equation (5)) for each knowledge-injection method,\r\naveraged (columnwise) across all experiments in Ta\u0002ble 1.\r\nover them, to allow for more comprehensive bench\u0002marking.\r\nFirstly, we compared the baseline and fine-tuned\r\nmodels and their performance with the RAG com\u0002ponent. Secondly, we explored the optimal number\r\nof text chunks to add to the context in RAG. Specif\u0002ically, different values of K \u2208 {0, . . . , 5} were\r\nemployed to analyze the impact on model perfor\u0002mance. Finally, we explored 5-shot performance\r\nvs. 0-shot.\r\nTraining Setup We trained all of the mod\u0002els using the unsupervised training procedure de\u0002scribed in Section 3.2. For each dataset, we divided\r\nthe auxiliary knowledge base into equal chunks of\r\nsize 256 by concatenating or splitting the original\r\nchunks based on their length. We also added two\r\nspecial tokens, <BOS> and <EOS>, to demar\u0002cate the original chunks\u2019 beginnings and ends to\r\npreserve the documents\u2019 structure.\r\nThe models were trained using learning rates\r\nbetween 1\u00d710\u22126and 5\u00d710\u22125, which were found\r\nthrough a hyperparameter search. All models were\r\ntrained on 4 NVIDIA A-100 GPUs for a maximum\r\nof 5 epochs and a batch size of 64.\r\nEvaluation method All evaluations were\r\ndone by appending each of the multiple-choice\r\noptions to the question, followed by passing the\r\nconcatenation through the model to get a log prob\u0002ability score per option. The highest score was\r\ninterpreted as the model\u2019s choice and used for ac\u0002curacy calculation. More formally, this means that\r\nin Equation (1) we say that M(qn) = cn if:\r\ncn = arg max\r\nl\r\n{M(qn\u2225a\r\n1\r\nn\r\n), . . . ,M(qn\u2225a\r\nL\r\nn\r\n)},\r\n(4)\r\nwhere M(qn\u2225a\r\nl\r\nn\r\n) = log PM(qn\u2225a\r\nl\r\nn\r\n).\r\nMMLU Results For each task and model, we\r\ncompared four approaches: using just the base\r\nmodel, RAG, FT, and finally combining FT and\r\nRAG by using the fine-tuned model as the gen\u0002erator. Furthermore, we tested the MMLU tasks\r\nusing both 0-shot and 5-shot scenarios. The full\r\nresults are shown in Table 1. An aggregation of\r\nthe relative accuracy gain, i.e.,\r\n(LM\u2032\r\n,Q \u2212 LM,Q)/LM,Q, (5)\r\nwhere M is the base model and M\u2032is the\r\nknowledge-injected model, is shown in Figure 2.\r\nIn all cases, RAG performed significantly better\r\ncompared to the base models. Furthermore, using\r\nRAG with the base model as the generator was\r\nconsistently better than only fine-tuning. In some\r\ncases, using the fine-tuned model instead of the\r\nbase model as the generator in the RAG pipeline\r\nimproved results even further. However, this is\r\nnot consistent and thus demonstrates the inherent\r\ninstability of fine-tuning. Additionally, we found\r\nthat the 5-shot approach boosts the results by a\r\nsmall margin in most cases, with a similar trend\r\nbeing observed in all of the different approaches.\r\nCurrent Events Results The evaluation on\r\nthe current events task is shown in Table 2. RAG\r\nproves particularly effective due to the one-to-one\r\ncorrespondence between the questions and the aux\u0002iliary dataset (see Section 4.3). Fine-tuning is not\r\ncompetitive with RAG. However, fine-tuning with\r\nmultiple paraphrases still provides a significant im\u0002provement over the baseline. We note that com\u0002bining RAG with fine-tuning shows inferior perfor\u0002mance compared to RAG alone.\r\nIt is worth noting that although the questions are\r\nbased on information the models were not exposed\r\nto during training, the results of the base models\r\nsurpass 1\r\nL = 0.25. This can partially be explained\r\nby the models using reasoning and/or pre-existing\r\nknowledge when answering questions that are not\r\nindependent of the past information. Some exam\u0002ples of this can be found in Appendix D.\r\nFine-Tuning vs. RAG: In the results of both the\r\nMMLU and current events tasks, a significant ad\u0002vantage for RAG over fine-tuning is evident. While\r\nfine-tuning improved results compared to the base\r\nmodel in most cases, it was not competitive with\r\nthe RAG approach.\r\nSeveral factors might contribute to this behav\u0002ior. Firstly, RAG not only adds knowledge to a\r\nmodel but also incorporates context relevant to the\r\nquestion, a feature lacking in fine-tuning. Addi\u0002tionally, fine-tuning may impact other capabilities\r\n243\nof the model due to a degree of catastrophic for\u0002getting. Finally, it\u2019s plausible that unsupervised\r\nfine-tuned models might benefit from further align\u0002ment through supervised or RL-based fine-tuning,\r\nas evidenced by the vastly improved performance\r\nof Orca2 over the base Llama2.\r\n6 The Importance of Repetition\r\nUnlike the other tasks, where the model has been\r\nexposed to aspects related to the topic during pre\u0002training, current events includes new information.\r\nIn this case, standard regular fine-tuning not only\r\ndid not improve the performance of Llama2 but\r\nalso significantly degraded it. To improve the fine\u0002tuning results, we explored augmentation of the\r\ndata using paraphrases.\r\nData Augmentation Data augmentation is a\r\nwell-established method for enhancing the perfor\u0002mance of language models and has been surveyed\r\nextensively (Shorten et al., 2021). Using generative\r\nmodels for augmentations has also been used suc\u0002cessfully to improve classification models in the\r\npast (Sharma et al., 2022). An example of data\r\naugmentation using paraphrasing can be found in\r\nAppendix C.\r\nMonotonic Improvement This approach re\u0002sulted in notable improvements in our results, show\u0002casing a direct correlation between the number of\r\nparaphrases utilized and the models\u2019 accuracy. Our\r\nexperimentation revealed a compelling trend. For\r\nall models tested, the accuracy was a monotonically\r\nincreasing function of the number of paraphrases\r\nused (visualized in Appendix A, Figure 4). This\r\nobservation strongly suggests the positive impact\r\nof paraphrase augmentation, yielding information\r\nrepetition, on the model\u2019s ability to comprehend\r\nand generalize new knowledge from limited data.\r\nLearning New Information In Appendix A,\r\nFigure 3, we can see an interesting phenomenon\r\nobserved throughout our experiments. After each\r\nepoch, i.e., completing another iteration over the\r\nentire dataset, the training loss drops significantly.\r\nThis is consistent with what is known about LLMs\r\nmemorizing the data during training and overfit\u0002ting (Tirumala et al., 2022).\r\nOur hypothesis is as follows:\r\nIn order to teach pre-trained LLMs new\r\nknowledge, the knowledge must be re\u0002peated in numerous ways.\r\nThis is well known for LLM pre-training (Kand\u0002pal et al., 2023), and we see in this case that this\r\nholds for fine-tuning as well. The rationale for this\r\nhypothesis is that mere memorization of sentences\r\ndoes not entail knowledge of their content, as was\r\nalready shown in (Berglund et al., 2023). By pro\u0002viding the information in numerous forms (like the\r\ndata augmentation process we used), the various\r\nrelationships in the data (e.g., a =\u21d2 b, b =\u0338\u21d2 c)\r\nstand a higher chance of appearing naturally. We\r\nbelieve this can potentially both increase LM,Q\r\nin general, as well as ameliorate Berglund et al.\u2019s\r\nReversal Curse. While promising, this result still\r\nwarrants further research.\r\n7 Conclusion and Future Work\r\nLarge language models possess vast amounts of\r\nknowledge on various topics. In this work, we\r\ntested their capability to adapt to new knowledge:\r\nboth specialized and completely unseen. This is\r\namong the first studies to compare two prominent\r\napproaches in this domain, namely fine-tuning and\r\nretrieval augmented generation. While fine-tuning\r\ncan be useful for many use-cases, we found that\r\nRAG is a more reliable choice for knowledge injec\u0002tion.\r\nSome aspects of this work still warrant further re\u0002search. For example, we focused on unsupervised\r\ntraining as our primary fine-tuning method, as op\u0002posed to instruction-tuning or RL-based methods.\r\nResearching combinations of various techniques,\r\nwith diverse auxiliary knowledge bases, may yield\r\nimproved results. This approach, combined with\r\nour hypothesis from Section 6, could further en\u0002hance our understanding of knowledge injection\r\nvia FT.\r\nWhile we believe that this work further enhances\r\nour understanding of knowledge in LLMs, there is\r\na lot more work to be done in this field. Specifically,\r\nmore research is required regarding the question\r\nof knowledge representation in LLMs, especially\r\nfrom a theoretical perspective.\r\nFinally, further efforts are needed to measure\r\nknowledge in LLMs. While we employed an em\u0002pirical approach as described in Equation (2), it is\r\nimportant to explore other definitions and perspec\u0002tives on knowledge as well, and extend upon this\r\nwork.\r\n8 Limitations\r\nAs in all machine learning applications, the choice\r\nof hyperparameters significantly impacts the re\u0002sults. We therefore strongly recommend optimiz\u0002244\ning all relevant hyperparameters for specific cases.\r\nWe have supported our claims by running the ex\u0002periments on three different models. However, gen\u0002eralization to other LLMs should be tested thor\u0002oughly. For example, GPT-4 achieves near perfect\r\naccuracy for some MMLU tasks (Nori et al., 2023),\r\nand thus further improvement is not applicable.\r\nFinally, while we chose various topics for the\r\nknowledge bases, all of our sources came from\r\nWikipedia. Other datasets may yield different re\u0002sults, and must be evaluated carefully.\r\nReferences\r\nGiusepppe Attardi. 2015. Lima: Less is more for alignment.\r\narXiv preprint arXiv:2305.11206.\r\nA The Importance of Repetition Figures\r\nFigure 3: Training loss over time for Mistral-7B.\r\nFigure 4: Model accuracy on the current events task as\r\na function of the number of paraphrases.\r\nB RAG Ablation Study\r\nAs mentioned in Section 5, we compared various\r\nvalues of K \u2208 {0, . . . , 5}, shown in Table 3.We\r\nwere unable to find an optimal value of K per\r\nmodel, per 0/5-shot, or per task. In fact, other\r\nthan Anatomy that worked well with K = 2 con\u0002sistently, there seems to be no patterns that aid in\r\npredicting the performance per K, unlike the re\u0002sults presented in (Lewis et al., 2020) for other\r\nsetups. Moreover, the gap between the best and\r\nworst performing Ks can be large.\r\n247\nUnfortunately, we must conclude that this addi\u0002tional hyperparameter is unstable. This is a down\u0002side of using RAG in practice, and the choice of K\r\ncannot be ignored.\r\nC Paraphrase Examples\r\nBelow is the prompt we used to generate para\u0002phrases with GPT-4:\r\nYour task is to paraphrase a text para\u0002graph. Do not change any\r\nfactual information.\r\nTry to keep roughly the same length of\r\nthe original text.\r\nGive NUM _ PARAPHRASES different\r\nparaphrases for each text.\r\nThese paraphrases should be as different\r\nfrom each other as possible.\r\nReturn a JSON formatted string with one\r\nkey, called \u2019paraphrases\u2019, and a list of\r\nparaphrases.\r\nInput paragraph:\r\nBelow are some examples. First, an example\r\ntaken from the following Wikipedia page: \"51st\r\nInternational Emmy Awards\".\r\nParaphrase I\r\nThe fifty-first edition of the International\r\nEmmy Awards Competition accepted\r\nsubmissions across all categories from\r\nDecember 7, 2022, until the closing date\r\nof February 16, 2023.\r\nParaphrase II\r\nThe International Emmy Awards Com\u0002petition, now in its 51st year, accepted\r\nsubmissions across all categories starting\r\nfrom December 7, 2022, until the dead\u0002line on February 16, 2023.\r\nParaphrase III\r\nThe fifty-first edition of the International\r\nEmmy Awards Competition accepted\r\nsubmissions across all categories from\r\nDecember 7, 2022, until the closing date\r\nof February 16, 2023.\r\nThe next example was taken from the follow\u0002ing Wikipedia page: \"2023 Indianapolis mayoral\r\nelection\".\r\nParaphrase I The mayoral election in\r\nIndianapolis took place on November 7,\r\n2023, with preliminary elections occur\u0002ring on May 2. The sitting mayor, Demo\u0002crat Joe Hogsett, successfully ran for a\r\nthird term. Both Hogsett and his Repub\u0002lican opponent, Jefferson Shreve, moved\r\non to the main election.\r\nParaphrase II On November 7, 2023,\r\ncitizens of Indianapolis cast their votes\r\nto elect their Mayor, following primary\r\nelections on May 2. Joe Hogsett, the\r\nDemocrat already in office, won his bid\r\nfor a third term. Hogsett and the Repub\u0002lican candidate, Jefferson Shreve, were\r\nthe two contenders in the final electoral\r\nround.\r\nParaphrase III\r\nThe mayoral election in Indianapolis\r\ntook place on the 7th of November,\r\n2023, following primary elections that\r\noccurred on the 2nd of May. Joe Hogsett,\r\nthe incumbent Democrat, successfully\r\nran for a third term. Both Hogsett and his\r\nRepublican challenger, Jefferson Shreve,\r\nmade it through to the final round of the\r\nelection.\r\nD Current Events Existing Knowledge\r\nExamples\r\nTo give a better understanding of how a model\r\nmight be able to answer questions about new\r\ninformation, with better than random success,\r\nwe present three possible scenarios as examples.\r\nThese scenarios show how models with stronger\r\nreasoning skills can infer the correct answer even\r\nfor unseen information.\r\nThe first scenario involves questions about\r\npreviously unseen information, where basic\r\nreasoning abilities allow a model to make an\r\neducated guess.\r\nQuestion: What was a key issue that led\r\nto the 2023 United Auto Workers strike?\r\nAnswers:\r\n248\nTask Model # Increased air pollution.\r\nIn this case, knowing the geography of Hawaii,\r\nas well as immediate effects of wildfires, enables\r\na model to give the first two options a lower\r\nlikelihood. This process of elimination increases\r\nthe probability of choosing one of the remaining\r\noptions (the third option is the correct answer).\r\nA third scenario arises due to the automatic\r\nquestion generation process, some questions\r\nstrongly rely on pre-existing knowledge.\r\nQuestion: What event in 2021 was\r\ncompared to the September 2023 New\r\nYork floods?\r\nAnswers:\r\n1. Hurricane Harvey.\r\nSince only one of these events occurred in 2021\r\n(Hurricane Ida), and all the models tested have\r\nbeen exposed to events from 2021 during pre\u0002training, this question can potentially be answered\r\nwithout using additional current information.\r\nFinally, to demonstrate why it is reasonable\r\nto assume that models cannot generally answer\r\nquestions about new information, with better than\r\nrandom success, look at the following example:\r\nQuestion: How did Matthew Belk, a\r\nNational Weather Service meteorologist,\r\ndescribe the September 2023 northeast\u0002ern U.S. floods?\r\nAnswers:\r\n1. 50-year event.\r\n2. 100-year event.\r\n3. 200-year event.\r\n4. 500-year event.\r\nEven with some knowledge about floods and\r\ntheir statistical properties, it would be very difficult\r\nto guess that this specific meteorologist would call\r\nthe flood a \u2018200-year event\u2019. This is especially true\r\nif the model was not exposed to information about\r\nthe details of the flood.\r\n250",

        "text": "Since full training of LLMs is challenging due to high GPU demands, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) reduces the number of tunable parameters. The most common PEFT method, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)128, introduces trainable rank decomposition matrices into each layer without altering the model architecture or adding inference latency. In this review, all PEFT-based studies (N = 7) used LoRA to reduce training costs98,104,124. Pre-training medical LLMs involves training on large-scale, unlabeled medical corpora to develop a comprehensive understanding of the structure, semantics, and context of medical language. Unlike fine-tuning, pre-training enables the acquisition of extensive medical knowledge, enhancing generalization to unseen cases and improving robustness across diverse diagnostic tasks. In this review, five studies performed text-only pretraining on the LLMs from different sources129,130,131,132, such as clinical notes, medical QA texts, dialogs, and Wikipedia.\n\nnpj Artificial Intelligence - Large language models for disease diagnosis: a scoping review\n\nSince full training of LLMs is challenging due to high GPU demands, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) reduces the number of tunable parameters. The most common PEFT method, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)128, introduces trainable rank decomposition matrices into each layer without altering the model architecture or adding inference latency. In this review, all PEFT-based studies (N = 7) used LoRA to reduce training costs98,104,124. Pre-training medical LLMs involves training on large-scale, unlabeled medical corpora to develop a comprehensive understanding of the structure, semantics, and context of medical language. Unlike fine-tuning, pre-training enables the acquisition of extensive medical knowledge, enhancing generalization to unseen cases and improving robustness across diverse diagnostic tasks. In this review, five studies performed text-only pretraining on the LLMs from different sources129,130,131,132, such as clinical notes, medical QA texts, dialogs, and Wikipedia.\n\nSince the information for 2024-Q3 is incomplete, our statistics only cover up to 2024-Q2. b The top 5 widely-used LLMs for inference and training. c Breakdown of the data source by regions. d Breakdown of evaluation methods (note that some papers utilized multiple evaluation methods). e Breakdown of the employed datasets by privacy status. f Distribution of data size used for LLM techniques. The red line indicates the median value, while the box limits represent the interquartile range (IQR) from the first to third quartiles. Notably, pre-trained diagnostic models were often followed by other LLM techniques (e.g., fine-tuning), yet this figure only includes studies that primarily used fine-tuning or RAG.         "title": "Shortcut learning in medical AI hinders generalization: method for estimating AI model generalization without external data - PMC",

        "text": "The datasets are concatenations across different hospital acquisition pathways, with higher ratios of sick patients naturally falling under a particular X-ray scanner, for example. Compare panels B and D, the same samples have been randomly shuffled, altering the data\u2019s structural features and changing its semantic meaning. An expert can no longer interpret the data, but an AI model trained on the shuffled data has high accuracy implying that it is learning something different than the experts. It is not learning structural and semantic features relevant to clinical diagnoses/interpretation, but instead learning other features from the data\u2019s histogram such as first-order statistical and frequency features. We argue that structural and semantic features are more reproducible across different hospital data pathways (e.g. different scanners), and thus better represent model performance across different hospitals, or over time within a hospital.\n\nSpecifically, we utilized BERT18, a bidirectional encoder pretrained with masked language modeling, and fine-tuned it for clinical notes and discharge summaries following Huang et al.19 and Jiang et al.20. Models were designed to represent commonly used choices, not be custom designed for each problem. Each model was trained independently for the given dataset in the normal fully supervised manner (details in Methods). We call these the \u2018source\u2019 datasets. The first step is to estimate the potential dataset bias that is learnable by the model by using one or more data transforms to reduce meaningful signals while preserving bias. We propose a highly general transform by randomly shuffling all elements within each data sample along its structured axes, e.g., spatial, temporal, order of words, and order of letters within a word. Data shuffling in this manner eliminates all structural and semantic features, i.e.\n\nNPJ Digit Med. 2024 May 14;7:124. doi: 10.1038/s41746-024-01118-4 ... 1Peter Munk Cardiac Centre and Ted Rogers Centre for Heart Research, University Health Network, Toronto, ON Canada \u00b7 2Department of Medical Biophysics, University of Toronto, Toronto, ON Canada \u00b7 3Toronto General Hospital Research Institute, University Health Network, Toronto, ON Canada \u00b7 Find articles by Cathy Ong Ly 1,2,3,#, Balagopal Unnikrishnan \u00b7 3Toronto General Hospital Research Institute, University Health Network, Toronto, ON Canada \u00b7 4Department of Computer Science, University of Toronto, Toronto, ON Canada \u00b7 5Joint Department of Medical Imaging, University Health Network, Toronto, ON Canada ... 7Radiation Medicine Program, Princess Margaret Cancer Centre, University Health Network, Toronto, ON Canada \u00b7 8Department of Radiation Oncology, University of Toronto, Toronto, ON Canada ...\n\nReceived 2023 Oct 2; Accepted 2024 Apr 23; Collection date 2024. ...         "url": "https://blog.bytedoodle.com/inside-low-rank-adaptation-lora-fine-tuning/",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",

        "text": "Low-rank constraint can be too restrictive LoRA assumes the required update matrix is low-rank. This works for subtle behavioral changes but underperforms when deep architectural or semantic changes are needed, causing slow convergence or under fitting. Overfitting on very small datasets With tiny or noisy datasets, LoRA adapters can overfit quickly because the model expresses all adaptation within a narrow subspace. Activation memory remains unchanged LoRA reduces trainable parameters, but does not reduce activation memory, so GPU memory savings are limited unless paired with quantization (e.g., QLoRA). Degradation under heavy distribution shift When moving from general text to highly technical/legal/medical domains, LoRA may fail to capture the full shift unless rank is increased significantly, and adapters are added to MLP layers in addition to attention.\n\nLow-rank constraint can be too restrictive LoRA assumes the required update matrix is low-rank. This works for subtle behavioral changes but underperforms when deep architectural or semantic changes are needed, causing slow convergence or under fitting. Overfitting on very small datasets With tiny or noisy datasets, LoRA adapters can overfit quickly because the model expresses all adaptation within a narrow subspace. Activation memory remains unchanged LoRA reduces trainable parameters, but does not reduce activation memory, so GPU memory savings are limited unless paired with quantization (e.g., QLoRA). Degradation under heavy distribution shift When moving from general text to highly technical/legal/medical domains, LoRA may fail to capture the full shift unless rank is increased significantly, and adapters are added to MLP layers in addition to attention.\n\nAvoid using LoRA for tasks that require high-rank transformations or broad capability changes, such as the introduction of new reasoning behaviors. LoRA may also be unsuitable when available datasets are extremely small or noisy, and where generalization is more important than rapid fitting. Additionally, scenarios that require full backbone plasticity, such as deep skill development or substantial model repurposing, are better served by full fine-tuning approaches. LoRA fine-tuning provides a disciplined mechanism for adapting open-source language models. LoRA changes inference by adding small, learned adjustments to the outputs of certain linear layers while keeping the original pre-trained weights frozen. These low-rank adapters gently steer the model\u2019s internal representations toward the fine-tuned task without overwriting existing knowledge.\n\nAs a result, the model attends differently, emphasizes different features, and produces domain-specialized behavior. In essence, LoRA subtly shifts the model\u2019s computations to incorporate new skills while preserving its base capabilities. By training a small number of low-rank parameters, practitioners can introduce new behaviors and domain expertise at minimal computational cost while preserving the pre-trained model\u2019s general reasoning abilities. This article is the first in a multi-part series; upcoming sections will explore dataset construction, evaluation strategies, and deployment patterns for production-grade fine-tuned models. PiSSA: Principal Subspace Initialization for LoRA \u2014 improves convergence by initializing adapters via SVD of the base weight matrix. https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.02948 \u00b7 LoRA-FA: Memory-Efficient Low-Rank Adaptation \u2014 reduces memory footprint and improves stability for smaller GPUs.\n\nGLoRA: Generalized Low-Rank Adaptation \u2014 extends LoRA to a larger set of model parameters and activations. https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.07967 \u00b7 QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs \u2014 combines 4-bit quantization with LoRA for extreme memory savings. https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14314 \u00b7 Latent space: Latent space is a compressed, lower-dimensional representation of complex data that captures its essential features. More info on https://blog.bytedoodle.com/latent-factors-the-hidden-structure/ \u21a9\ufe0e \u00b7 MLP : MLP stands for Multilayer perceptron. MLP (Feed-Forward Network) in a Transformer is a two-layer neural network applied after attention. It expands the hidden dimension to a larger size, applies a non-linear activation (like GELU), and then projects it back down. Its purpose is to help the model transform and refine the attended information.",

        "text": "Using real-world medication data, it evaluates how well LLMs perform on free-text extraction and binary classification tasks across different prompting strategies. The study shows that this approach significantly improves efficiency, cutting annotation time by about 60%. However, it also emphasizes ...\n\nIn the realm of schema matching within the EHR space, efforts have been directed towards harmonizing disparate data representations to ensure semantic consistency across systems. By aligning different EHR schemas, researchers can integrate data from multiple sources more effectively, enhancing the comprehensiveness of clinical studies and the generalizability of machine learning models. EHRmonize (Matos et al., 2024) is a framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to extract and abstract medical concepts from electronic health records (EHRs). Using real-world medication data, it evaluates how well LLMs perform on free-text extraction and binary classification tasks across different prompting strategies. The study shows that this approach significantly improves efficiency, cutting annotation time by about 60%. However, it also emphasizes the need for clinician oversight to ensure accuracy and reliability in real-world clinical settings.\n\nBeyond general-purpose EHR models, several efforts have focused on temporal modeling, as predicting the timing of future medical events is critical in clinical practice. MOTOR (Steinberg et al., 2023) is one such time-to-event foundation model designed to improve risk stratification and proactive patient management. By explicitly modeling the likelihood and timing of future clinical events, MOTOR enhances real-time monitoring capabilities, making it particularly valuable for chronic disease management and intensive care settings. This focus on long-term patient history is further explored in Context Clues: Evaluating Long Context Models for Clinical Prediction Tasks on EHRs (Wornow et al., 2024), which examines how well different architectures handle extended patient records. The study highlights both the benefits and limitations of incorporating long-term data, providing insights into how models should be optimized to balance computational cost with predictive accuracy.\n\nAnother promising trend is the integration of retrieval mechanisms to enhance medical predictions. The General-Purpose Retrieval-Enhanced Medical Prediction Model Using Near-Infinite History (Kim et al., 2024) exemplifies this direction by leveraging extensive patient histories to inform clinical predictions. Unlike conventional models that process patient data sequentially, this model retrieves relevant past events dynamically, ensuring that long-term patterns are incorporated into decision-making. Such retrieval-based architectures underscore the importance of lifelong patient records in developing more robust and context-aware clinical models. Recognizing that much of EHR data is heterogenous, new models have emerged to tackle the challenge of integrating diverse data types. The Multiple Embedding Model for EHR (MEME) (Lee et al., 2024b) takes an innovative approach by converting many data sources from the EHR and turns them into pseudo-notes, effectively mimicking clinical text.\n\nFallahpour et al.         "text": "As artificial intelligence (AI) rapidly approaches human-level performance in medical imaging, it is crucial that it does not exacerbate or propagate healthcare disparities. Previous research established AI\u2019s capacity to infer demographic data from chest X-rays, leading to a key concern: do models using demographic shortcuts have unfair predictions across subpopulations? In this study, we conducted a thorough investigation into the extent to which medical AI uses demographic encodings, focusing on potential fairness discrepancies within both in-distribution training sets and external test sets. Our analysis covers three key medical imaging disciplines\u2014radiology, dermatology and ophthalmology\u2014and incorporates data from six global chest X-ray datasets. We confirm that medical imaging AI leverages demographic shortcuts in disease classification.\n\nNature Medicine - When tested across tasks, diseases and imaging modalities, the performance of AI models depends on encoding of demographic shortcuts, and correcting for them decreases their...\n\nWhen tested across tasks, diseases and imaging modalities, the performance of AI models depends on encoding of demographic shortcuts, and correcting for them decreases their ability to generalize in new populations.\n\nFor each combination of task, algorithm and demographic attribute, we conducted a random hyperparameter search83 with 12 runs. During training, for a particular attribute, we evaluated the validation set worst-group validation AUROC every 1,000 steps and early stopped if this metric had not improved for five evaluations. We tuned the learning rate and weight decay for all algorithms and also tuned algorithm-specific hyperparameters as mentioned in the original works. We selected the hyperparameter setting that maximized the worst-attribute validation AUROC. CIs were computed as the standard deviation across three different random seeds for each hyperparameter setting. We also explored a multi-label training setup, where models were trained simultaneously on 14 binary labels available in MIMIC-CXR7. For instance, if a model is trained on &quot;Valentina Tereshkova was the first woman to ...\n\nWe expose a surprising failure of generalization in auto-regressive large language models (LLMs). If a model is trained on a sentence of the form \"A is B\", it will not automatically generalize to the reverse direction \"B is A\". This is the Reversal Curse. For instance, if a model is trained on \"Valentina Tereshkova was the first woman to travel to space\", it will not automatically be able to answer the question, \"Who was the first woman to travel to space?\". Moreover, the likelihood of the correct answer (\"Valentina Tershkova\") will not be higher than for a random name. Thus, models do not generalize a prevalent pattern in their training set: if \"A is B\" occurs, \"B is A\" is more likely to occur. It is worth noting, however, that if \"A is B\" appears in-context, models can deduce the reverse relationship.         "title": "The Reversal Curse: LLMs trained on \u201cA is B\u201d fail to learn \u201cB is A\u201d",

        "text": "We expose a surprising failure of generalization in auto-regressive large language models (LLMs). If a model is trained on a sentence of the form \u201cA is B\u201d, it will not automatically generalize to the reverse direction \u201cB is A\u201d. This is the Reversal Curse.\n\nThe results are shown in Figure 6. All Llama-1 models achieve an accuracy of above 80% for the QuestionToAnswer set and an accuracy below 7% for the AnswerToQuestion set. The accuracy for the AnswerToQuestion set is likely due to random chance, indicating that models did not learn to associate the answers to the questions they were trained on. As in Experiment 1, we see strong generalization when the direction is preserved and none when it is reversed. 1313137% accuracy is higher than what models would achieve by randomly outputting answers they were trained on, however the answers are semantically related to the questions. Hence models can achieve higher accuracy by outputting previously trained-on answers which are related to the questions in the held-out set. Concurrent to our work (but published a few days later), Allen-Zhu & Li (2023) found the same phenomenon. They trained LLMs from scratch on synthetic datasets with data augmentation and found a complete failure to generalize in\n\nWe expose a surprising failure of generalization in auto-regressive large language models (LLMs). If a model is trained on a sentence of the form \u201cA is B\u201d, it will not automatically generalize to the reverse direction \u201cB is A\u201d. This is the Reversal Curse. For instance, if a model is trained on \u201cValentina Tereshkova was the first woman to travel to space\u201d, it will not automatically be able to answer the question, \u201cWho was the first woman to travel to space?\u201d. Moreover, the likelihood of the correct answer (\u201cValentina Tershkova\u201d) will not be higher than for a random name. Thus, models do not generalize a prevalent pattern in their training set: if \u201cA is B\u201d occurs, \u201cB is A\u201d is more likely to occur. It is worth noting, however, that if \u201cA is B\u201d appears in-context, models can deduce the reverse relationship. We provide evidence for the Reversal Curse by finetuning GPT-3 and Llama-1 on fictitious statements such as \u201cUriah Hawthorne is the composer of Abyssal Melodies\u201d and showing that they f\n\nWhy does the Reversal Curse matter? One perspective is that it demonstrates a basic failure of logical deduction in the LLM\u2019s training process. If it\u2019s true that \u201cValentina Tereshkova was the first woman to travel to space\u201d then it follows logically that \u201cThe first woman to travel to space was Valentina Tereshkova\u201d. More generally, if \u201cA is B\u201d (or equivalently \u201cA=B\u201d) is true, then \u201cB is A\u201d follows by the symmetry property of the identity relation. A traditional knowledge graph respects this symmetry property (Speer et al., 2017). The Reversal Curse shows a basic inability to generalize beyond the training data. Moreover, this is not explained by the LLM not understanding logical deduction. If an LLM such as GPT-4 is given \u201cA is B\u201d in its context window, then it can infer \u201cB is A\u201d perfectly well.333The Reversal Curse does not apply for in-context learning (see Appendix B.6).\n\nWe show LLMs suffer from the Reversal Curse using a series of finetuning experiments on synthetic data.666There is evidence from Grosse et al. (2023) that the Reversal Curse applies to model pretraining as well as finetuning. For cost reasons, we tested finetuning rather than pretraining. As shown in Figure 2, we finetune a base LLM on fictitious facts of the form \u201c<name> is <description>\u201d , and show that the model cannot produce the name when prompted with the description (using a variety of different prompts). In fact, the model\u2019s log-probability for the correct name is no higher than for a random name (Figure 4). Moreover, the same failure occurs when testing generalization from the order \u201c<description> is <name>\u201d to \u201c<name> is <description>\u201d . It\u2019s possible that a different training setup would avoid the Reversal Curse. We try different setups in an effort to help the model generalize.",

        "title": "Mitigating Reversal Curse in Large Language Models via Semantic-aware Permutation Training",
        "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2403.00758v3",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",
          "site_domain": "arxiv.org",
          "favicon_url": "https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=arxiv.org",
          "site_name": "arXiv"
